## Psalms 41  ·  Primary study  ·  2026-09-26

Source: https://t.me/HouseOfWews2/17873

<!-- post 17873 · 2026-09-26 07:31 · scripture -->

**Psalms 41**
*Blessed is he that considereth the poor: the LORD will deliver him in time of trouble.*

*2 The LORD will preserve him, and keep him alive; and he shall be blessed upon the earth: and thou wilt not deliver him unto the will of his enemies.*

*3 The LORD will strengthen him upon the bed of languishing: thou wilt make all his bed in his sickness.*

*4 I said, LORD, be merciful unto me: heal my soul; for I have sinned against thee.*

*5 Mine enemies speak evil of me, When shall he die, and his name perish?*

*6 And if he come to see me, he speaketh vanity: his heart gathereth iniquity to itself; when he goeth abroad, he telleth it.*

*7 All that hate me whisper together against me: against me do they devise my hurt.*

*8 An evil disease, say they, cleaveth fast unto him: and now that he lieth he shall rise up no more.*

*9 Yea, mine own familiar friend, in whom I trusted, which did eat of my bread, hath lifted up his heel against me.*

*10 But thou, O LORD, be merciful unto me, and raise me up, that I may requite them.*

*11 By this I know that thou favourest me, because mine enemy doth not triumph over me.*

*12 And as for me, thou upholdest me in mine integrity, and settest me before thy face for ever.*

*13 Blessed be the LORD God of Israel from everlasting, and to everlasting. Amen, and Amen.*

<!-- post 17874 · 2026-09-26 07:48 · study -->

**Psalm 41** is brief and to the point. David opens with the doctrine of the Lord's promise to preserve and give long life on the earth to those of His people who consider the poor. Who show mercy to the widows and take up the cause of the fatherless. 

Just as the commandment to honor your parents comes with a promise, that it be well with you and lengthen your days, this practice of pure religion, to consider the poor, comes with a similar blessing. 

Basic stuff, which we are told over and over again in scripture, because it often is the hardest thing for man to do; love the unlovable the way God loves us. 

**Luke 14:13–14**
*But when thou makest a feast, call the poor, the maimed, the lame, the blind: And thou shalt be blessed; for they cannot recompense thee: for thou shalt be recompensed at the resurrection of the just*.

The act of giving alms or feeding the poor is not itself what gives us blessings. Many may be generous in their actions, to be seen of men or to make themselves self-righteous. 

Helping people who cannot help you back out of love, is love unfeigned. It is the charity Paul calls us to. The selfless, sacrificial love for God and others, in your heart. More so than the actual alms or donation of goods. The spirit behind it is everything. 

**1 Corinthians 13:3**
*And though I bestow all my goods to feed the poor, and though I give my body to be burned, and have not charity, it profiteth me nothing.*

So we see that the love behind the care given to the poor, is rewarded with strengthening us in our sickness, delivering us from our enemies, God showing to us the mercy we showed those who could never repay us for the kindness. 

Those that do it for their own reasons, have their reward. But the heart behind it is what God wants from us. 

**Matthew 25:40**
*And the King shall answer and say unto them, Verily I say unto you, Inasmuch as ye have done it unto one of the least of these my brethren, ye have done it unto me*.

**Proverbs 19:17**
*He that hath pity upon the poor lendeth unto the Lord; and that which he hath given will he pay him again.*

God loves a cheerful giver, one who isn't seeking anything for himself. 

**Proverbs 22:9**
*He that hath a bountiful eye shall be blessed; for he giveth of his bread to the poor.*

But those that oppress the poor, God hates. 

**Proverbs 14:31**
*He that oppresseth the poor reproacheth his Maker: but he that honoureth him hath mercy on the needy*

<!-- post 17875 · 2026-09-26 08:00 · study -->

David establishes this truth early in the psalm for a reason, which is made clear as it goes along. 

It isn't so much as David brags about his charitable heart to the poor, but in the opening verses, he humbly reminds himself and the Lord he is petitioning of the promises He has made. 

Then, the prayer moves to confession. The honest assessment of the heart before a holy, holy, holy God. 

This pattern of reminders and confession, before petition, is helpful to keep in mind for our prayer life, if we want it to be successful. 

We often hear about "prayer warriors," those who are continuously in prayer, especially for others. Who seek the burdens of others to bring them before the Lord, and it is a wonderful work. 

But as we seek to be counted among the elite forces of prayer, we should always keep in mind the humility and confession that makes our prayer life effective. 

And so when David writes *be merciful unto me: heal my soul; for I have sinned against thee*, we need to take it to heart in our own approach to the Throne. The man who was the apple of God's eye was honest in his confession. The King of Israel was lowly enough to be convicted as he approached the Throne of Grace. 

Watch out for the creeping pride that may come from being recognized as a "prayer warrior," and the audacity that may infect what is a great habit. The loss of the love of others that should drive it, but iniquity creeping in. 

Begin with confession. A contrite heart with love unfeigned can move mountains.

<!-- post 17876 · 2026-09-26 08:11 · study -->

The remainder of **Psalm 41** gives context, a sickly sorrow from illness has come. And in his weakness, David sees the opportunists and haters using his failed health to speak ill against him. 

They are backbiters, praising Him with vanities and flattery to his face, but quietly questioning "when will he die?" and when can they take over. Cursing him by claiming God was punishing him.

*All that hate me whisper together against me: against me do they devise my hurt. An evil disease, say they, cleaveth fast unto him: and now that he lieth he shall rise up no more.*

He faces betrayal and evil, even as the misery of illness besets him. 

So, David has reminded God of His healing promises for considering the poor, he has confessed his sins before God. He has laid out the problem he faces, and then asks for God's help to deliver him. 

He pleas for the Lord to be merciful. To remember His promises and forgiveness. And asks for this petition to be answered that David may know he is favored by the grace shown to him. That the evil men do not triumph, and give them cause to scoff those that trust in the name of the Lord. 

Finally, he closes this prayerful lament with the truth that God has upheld him, and saved him, forever. His salvation remains regardless of health or men's evil or anything else that God wills. And with that blessed hope, he closes in praise for the Almighty. 

*Blessed be the LORD God of Israel from everlasting, and to everlasting. Amen, and Amen.*

<!-- post 17877 · 2026-09-26 08:25 · study -->

During his low point of illness and enemies scheming, David mentions betrayal.

*Yea, mine own familiar friend, in whom I trusted, which did eat of my bread, hath lifted up his heel against me.*

The truth is that we often face betrayal at the most inopportune times, when we are at our lowest. Or even those we trusted and rely on, letting us down when we need them the most. 

This truth is one of the most devastating things we grapple with in life. Not that there are bad people who seek our harm, but there are close friends, even brothers, who turn on us in our darkest hours. 

*But thou, O LORD, be merciful unto me, and raise me up, that I may requite them.*

Jesus explicitly quotes **Psalm 41:9** during the Last Supper in **John 13:18**;
*He that eateth bread with me hath lifted up his heel against me.* 

David’s specific pain was a literal prophecy of Christ’s betrayal by Judas. Jesus knows our infirmities, He has suffered our pains. He knows what it is like to be betrayed by those closest to Him. (Not only the evil of Judas, but also being denied by Peter, who loves Him)

But, thanks be to God that He is steadfast and true. The Lord raises us up, and gives us the strength to overcome their evil, that it all turn out good, because He loves us. 

**Proverbs 18:24**
*A man that hath friends must shew himself friendly: and there is a friend that sticketh closer than a brother*.

That friend is Jesus Christ. 

Even our family may turn against us.

**Matthew 10:34-36**
*Think not that I am come to send peace on earth: I came not to send peace, but a sword. For I am come to set a man at variance against his father, and the daughter against her mother, and the daughter in law against her mother in law. And a man’s foes shall be they of his own household.*

But we have the assurance and knowledge that He will always be there for us. *For he hath said, I will never leave thee, nor forsake thee*.

**Deuteronomy 31:6**
*Be strong and of a good courage, fear not, nor be afraid of them: for the Lord thy God, he it is that doth go with thee; he will not fail thee, nor forsake thee.*

We can take comfort in Jesus' own promise to us:…*and, lo, I am with you alway, even unto the end of the world. Amen.*

Amen.

## Psalms 42  ·  Primary study  ·  2026-09-27

Source: https://t.me/HouseOfWews2/17879

<!-- post 17879 · 2026-09-27 06:31 · scripture -->

**Psalms 42**
*As the hart panteth after the water brooks, so panteth my soul after thee, O God.*

*2 My soul thirsteth for God, for the living God: when shall I come and appear before God?*

*3 My tears have been my meat day and night, while they continually say unto me, Where is thy God?*

*4 When I remember these things, I pour out my soul in me: for I had gone with the multitude, I went with them to the house of God, with the voice of joy and praise, with a multitude that kept holyday.*

*5 Why art thou cast down, O my soul? and why art thou disquieted in me? hope thou in God: for I shall yet praise him for the help of his countenance.*

*6 O my God, my soul is cast down within me: therefore will I remember thee from the land of Jordan, and of the Hermonites, from the hill Mizar.*

*7 Deep calleth unto deep at the noise of thy waterspouts: all thy waves and thy billows are gone over me.*

*8 Yet the LORD will command his lovingkindness in the day time, and in the night his song shall be with me, and my prayer unto the God of my life.*

*9 I will say unto God my rock, Why hast thou forgotten me? why go I mourning because of the oppression of the enemy?*

*10 As with a sword in my bones, mine enemies reproach me; while they say daily unto me, Where is thy God?*

*11 Why art thou cast down, O my soul? and why art thou disquieted within me? hope thou in God: for I shall yet praise him, who is the health of my countenance, and my God.*

<!-- post 17880 · 2026-09-27 07:06 · study -->

**Psalm 42** is a gut-punch of a song, a prayer of desperation from a troubled believer, being strengthened in their faith, while simultaneously going through the wringer. 

The psalmists (this psalm is attributed to the "Sons of Korah," likely the musicians and singers in the Temple) write of a man whose soul is panting after the Lord, as a hart, or deer, pants after the water of a brook. The thirst of need. 

*My soul thirsteth for God, for the living God: when shall I come and appear before God?*

A plaintive lament, needing the Lord so badly that we'd like to die, just to be with Him and at peace. *Tears have been my meat,* the psalmist says. All he has consumed, day and night, while scoffers say, *Where is thy God?*

The spiritual thirst, though tears have fed him, is a powerful metaphor. But we have the promise of living waters, in which we shall never thirst. The promise that our spirit will be quenched in Christ. 

**John 4:14**
*But whosoever drinketh of the water that I shall give him shall never thirst; but the water that I shall give him shall be in him a well of water springing up into everlasting life.*

This promise is true and we can count on it, but the trials of this life can leave us panting for the living water, to be satisfied by them in the thirst we develop during low points in our lives. 

The psalmist writes of the remembrance of his joy in the Lord, the times he sang and worshipped in the congregation during holidays, and asks himself why is he so down and depressed. He knows he will yet praise God, when He turns His face towards him. 

Knowing that in the past, he's been delivered and trusting it will happen again, isn't just a comfort but in a way, a condemnation against himself. 

"Why am I so down hearted in my soul, when I can remember and appreciate all God has done for me, and my faith that he will again?" This is a place that even a mature Christian can come to. It is the truth that the weariness of this life can beat us down, even with unshakable faith. 

As Pastor Jack Hyles often preached, you can't have unshakable faith unless your faith has been shaken. 

The troubles become the source for the strength of our conviction. Because in trouble we are shown His power. 

And in our darkest moments, the voices of the scoffers cut like a knife. Satan uses them to attack our faith, as he sought to shake Job's. 

**Job 1:8–11**
*And the LORD said unto Satan, Hast thou considered my servant Job, that there is none like him in the earth, a perfect and an upright man, one that feareth God, and escheweth evil? Then Satan answered the LORD, and said, Doth Job fear God for nought? Hast not thou made an hedge about him, and about his house, and about all that he hath on every side? thou hast blessed the work of his hands, and his substance is increased in the land. But put forth thine hand now, and touch all that he hath, and he will curse thee to thy face.*

The mocking of the unrighteous, who delight to see God's children stumble, to say "Aha" when we fall, stems from this same desire Satan has, when he accused the brethren day and night before the Lord. Seeking whom he may devour. 

But we have the peace of God in tribulation, even if we are despondent in the moment, we know He is true. 

**Romans 5:1–6**
*Therefore being justified by faith, we have peace with God through our Lord Jesus Christ: By whom also we have access by faith into this grace wherein we stand, and rejoice in hope of the glory of God. And not only so, but we glory in tribulations also: knowing that tribulation worketh patience; And patience, experience; and experience, hope: And hope maketh not ashamed; because the love of God is shed abroad in our hearts by the Holy Ghost which is given unto us. For when we were yet without strength, in due time Christ died for the ungodly.*

We were delivered from death into new life, at our worst. The hope we have in Christ will never leave us ashamed. Let them laugh us to scorn, we shall be saved and the Lord will cast them down.

<!-- post 17881 · 2026-09-27 07:06 · study -->

In our lowest, let us remember Him. Let our faith be spread across the land. Let us cry out to those that doubt, that our God is our rock and our refuge. 

But the light of the Lord can still seem dim and distant when our heart is troubled. We can acknowledge our own pain and suffering, and cry out to the Lord during it, that He may use it to purify ourselves. 

**1 Peter 4:13**
*But rejoice, inasmuch as ye are partakers of Christ's sufferings; that, when his glory shall be revealed, ye may be glad also with exceeding joy.*

Suffering leads to glory.

<!-- post 17882 · 2026-09-27 07:12 · study -->

*Deep calleth unto deep,* our soul, overwhelmed by sorrow, drowning in sadness, calls out to the Lord. 

*Yet the LORD will command his lovingkindness in the day time, and in the night his song shall be with me, and my prayer unto the God of my life.*

This hope is the anchor to our soul. Even when we feel He has forgotten us. 

The prayer acknowledging God is our rock, even as we ask "Why?" Why is it that we continue in sorrow and are oppressed by our enemies? Those who afflict us in our grief, and cast doubt on our faith and the power of our God, while we wallow in tears. We may seek the Lord, and ask Him "Why?" 

But in the end, while we may be down on ourselves for letting our depression and sadness get us down, we can trust in the faithfulness of God. 

*Why art thou cast down, O my soul? and why art thou disquieted within me? hope thou in God: for I shall yet praise him, who is the health of my countenance, and my God.* Amen.

<!-- post 17883 · 2026-09-27 07:24 · study -->

*Why art thou cast down, O my soul?* It is a comfort to any believer to realize that remembering past deliverances doesn't automatically cure present depression. 

We may take the moment of our pain and beat ourselves up for not appreciating what God has done for us. For allowing sadness and heartache to linger. Or that sorrow to affect us in ways that makes everything worse, when our own memories of past deliverance aren't enough to make us snap out of it, and we question and blame ourselves. 

True conviction is forged precisely when the storms are howling. Faith isn't the absence of trouble; it is the stubborn decision to anchor oneself to the Rock when you feel like you are drowning. 

Every life has seasons of strife. We shouldn't wallow in self-pity, but we can recognize our need for working through grief without beating ourselves up for it. 

We know the truth of Christ. We will sing His praise again. This too shall pass. But in the moment, it's okay to *say unto God my rock, Why hast thou forgotten me?* Just don't take this feeling as an excuse to forget God.

## Psalms 43  ·  Primary study  ·  2026-09-28

Source: https://t.me/HouseOfWews2/17888

<!-- post 17888 · 2026-09-28 05:25 · scripture -->

**Psalms 43**
*Judge me, O God, and plead my cause against an ungodly nation: O deliver me from the deceitful and unjust man.*

*2 For thou art the God of my strength: why dost thou cast me off? why go I mourning because of the oppression of the enemy?*

*3 O send out thy light and thy truth: let them lead me; let them bring me unto thy holy hill, and to thy tabernacles.*

*4 Then will I go unto the altar of God, unto God my exceeding joy: yea, upon the harp will I praise thee, O God my God.*

*5 Why art thou cast down, O my soul? and why art thou disquieted within me? hope in God: for I shall yet praise him, who is the health of my countenance, and my God.*

<!-- post 17889 · 2026-09-28 05:51 · study -->

**Psalm 43** is a shorter song of the same theme as **Psalm 42,** a condensed appeal to the Lord in desperate times. 

The call to judge, that is to be a deliverer, an advocate, to bring the cause of the prayerful against the ungodly that afflict them, is the heart of it. 

We may not be in the straits of captivity or war, or even persecution by our own government for the cause of Christ, and praise God for sparing us, but modern American Christians can find ourselves in calamity and a spiritual siege. 

**Ephesians 6:12**
*For we wrestle not against flesh and blood, but against principalities, against powers, against the rulers of the darkness of this world, against spiritual wickedness in high places.*

We may face opposition by the ungodly in our families, workplace, neighborhoods, even in some churches, anywhere that we find our faith and longsuffering tested by the slings and arrows of those who hate us and our Lord. Jesus warned us. 

**John 15:18**
*If the world hate you, ye know that it hated me before it hated you.*

He warned us that He would bring division, even among family.

**Luke 12:51–53**
*Suppose ye that I am come to give peace on earth? I tell you, Nay; but rather division: For from henceforth there shall be five in one house divided, three against two, and two against three. The father shall be divided against the son, and the son against the father; the mother against the daughter, and the daughter against the mother; the mother in law against her daughter in law, and the daughter in law against her mother in law.*

In any of this, the lack of scale and immediate physical danger doesn't change the fact that our heart can grow despondent over our circumstances, and the spiritual battles can be as consequential as the real world wars, at times. 

Reminding ourselves that Jesus is the God of our strength, the Rock of our salvation, even when we feel far from Him, is a common prayer in these Psalms, and with good reason. 

In **Zechariah 1:3**, the Bible says *Turn ye unto me, saith the Lord of hosts, and I will turn unto you*. A promise of God, repeated in the Epistles;

**James 4:7–8**
*Submit yourselves therefore to God. Resist the devil, and he will flee from you. Draw nigh to God, and he will draw nigh to you. Cleanse your hands, ye sinners; and purify your hearts, ye double minded.*

The reminder to come to God in humble confession is key, but the overall concept that God seeks our repentance **towards** Him, that He will close the gap and draw nigh is a fundamental doctrine, and a hope we see promised and delivered time and again in scripture. 

And in our peril, and in our desperate need, if we turn to God and humble ourselves before Him, and ask for His judgement, He will come to us and lift us up in His arms, and lead us to where we need to be. 

By His wonderful light and truth, we know the way to Him. Jesus said He is the way, the truth and the life, the light of the world, guiding us to His holy hill, the tents of His people, the mansions of glory. 

By the gospel shared among the people, the desperate are led to the house of God. To the local church, where His praises are sung in our deliverance, and where mourning may turn to joy. 

*Then will I go unto the altar of God, unto God my exceeding joy: yea, upon the harp will I praise thee, O God my God.*

The definitive *Then will I go*, shows the confidence in His promise. But the doubts of the flesh can cause us to be cast down. Depressed at the situation and wondering when God's hand will lift us up. The psalmist reconciles these two things in the person's spirit, this contradiction between the heart and the spirit, with the encouragement to hope in God. 

To know *Then will I go* because He is faithful and true. That no matter what comes, we shall praise Him. Here in the congregation, or in the heavenly choir. He is our God. Amen

<!-- post 17890 · 2026-09-28 06:12 · study -->

**Psalms 42 and 43** share the refrain;

*Why art thou cast down, O my soul? and why art thou disquieted within me? hope thou in God: for I shall yet praise him, who is the health of my countenance, and my God.*

And there are no attributions given to **Psalm 43** in the Hebrew, the way **Psalm 42** is attributed to the "sons of Korah." 

Some translations and traditions combine the two songs, to make it one continuous Psalm. 

While no one can say with any certainty, I believe the KJV translators were wise and Spirit guided to keep them separated. While the themes are similar as far as the broken-hearted nature of them, and the repeated refrain, they each carry a weight and purpose unique to themselves. 

The longer appeal in **Psalm 42** is more of a meditation on the despondency of man in these circumstances, while the more brief and succinct **Psalm 43** tips the balance more to the understanding of God's promises and the faith that overcomes the fear. 

Yes we still find the lament of our diminished spirit, but the focus has shifted slightly to the certain hope. 

It's brief nature makes it a more accessible devotional and gives someone who is coming out of depression a more focused resolution. 

But, taken together the last several Psalms show that the poor of spirit are exactly who God seeks to lift up. That His promises aren't only for the super-faithful warriors, but the meek and lowly. 

It is good to have a God Who understands our infirmities, and His Word as a relatable resource in our time of need.

**Psalms 44** 
*We have heard with our ears, O God, our fathers have told us, what work thou didst in their days, in the times of old.*

*4 Thou art my King, O God: command deliverances for Jacob.*

*5 Through thee will we push down our enemies: through thy name will we tread them under that rise up against us.*

*6 For I will not trust in my bow, neither shall my sword save me.*

*7 But thou hast saved us from our enemies, and hast put them to shame that hated us.*

*8 In God we boast all the day long, and praise thy name for ever. Selah.*

*9 But thou hast cast off, and put us to shame; and goest not forth with our armies.*

*10 Thou makest us to turn back from the enemy: and they which hate us spoil for themselves.*

*11 Thou hast given us like sheep appointed for meat; and hast scattered us among the heathen.*

*12 Thou sellest thy people for nought, and dost not increase thy wealth by their price.*

*13 Thou makest us a reproach to our neighbours, a scorn and a derision to them that are round about us.*

*14 Thou makest us a byword among the heathen, a shaking of the head among the people.*

*15 My confusion is continually before me, and the shame of my face hath covered me,*

*16 For the voice of him that reproacheth and blasphemeth; by reason of the enemy and avenger.*

*17 All this is come upon us; yet have we not forgotten thee, neither have we dealt falsely in thy covenant.*

*18 Our heart is not turned back, neither have our steps declined from thy way;*

*19 Though thou hast sore broken us in the place of dragons, and covered us with the shadow of death.*

*20 If we have forgotten the name of our God, or stretched out our hands to a strange god;*

*21 Shall not God search this out? for he knoweth the secrets of the heart.*

*22 Yea, for thy sake are we killed all the day long; we are counted as sheep for the slaughter.*

*23 Awake, why sleepest thou, O Lord? arise, cast us not off for ever.*

*24 Wherefore hidest thou thy face, and forgettest our affliction and our oppression?*

*25 For our soul is bowed down to the dust: our belly cleaveth unto the earth.*

*26 Arise for our help, and redeem us for thy mercies’ sake.*

<!-- post 17922 · 2026-10-02 05:31 · study -->

**Psalm 44** is attributed to *the sons of Korah*, a Levitical family of temple musicians and gatekeepers. Faithful singers, who are credited with 12 Psalms in total, 42-49 and 84-85, 87-88, across multiple generations. 

**Psalm 44** itself is thought to be written during the Assyrian invasion and their siege of Jerusalem and general destruction of the Southern Kingdom of Judea. 

It follows a similar pattern of congregational laments, as we saw in **Psalm 42-43**, and stresses the victories of the past that God has won for His people, while calling out for deliverance in the present. 

It opens with an acknowledgement of their knowledge of God's grace and might for His people, passed down by their fathers. 

*Our fathers have told us, what work thou didst in their days*. The importance of generational evangelism is incredibly important, for obvious reasons, we should tell our children about the importance of God moving in our lives, so that they may learn to trust in Him during hard times, and not be dismayed when tribulation comes.

But the practice of children asking and fathers telling was legally commanded in the Law of Moses to ensure that the memory of the Exodus from Egypt was never forgotten.

**Deuteronomy 6:18–23**
*And thou shalt do that which is right and good in the sight of the LORD: that it may be well with thee, and that thou mayest go in and possess the good land which the LORD sware unto thy fathers, To cast out all thine enemies from before thee, as the LORD hath spoken. And when thy son asketh thee in time to come, saying, What mean the testimonies, and the statutes, and the judgments, which the LORD our God hath commanded you? Then thou shalt say unto thy son, We were Pharaoh’s bondmen in Egypt; and the LORD brought us out of Egypt with a mighty hand: And the LORD shewed signs and wonders, great and sore, upon Egypt, upon Pharaoh, and upon all his household, before our eyes: And he brought us out from thence, that he might bring us in, to give us the land which he sware unto our fathers.*

The Psalm is not only stating their education as a fact, but is telling God in their prayer that their fathers obeyed and they listened, following His decree. That they were obedient in this thing He had said. 

The victories they were given by His power and promise and by the light of His countenance - His divine grace towards them, is remembered and appreciated. That their fathers didn't trust their own swords, or their own strength, but wholly relied on the Lord, *because thou hadst a favour unto them*.

When we are in straits, and feeling hopeless, the remembrance of past deliverance, coupled with the thankful acknowledgement to the Lord for these things, leads the prayer, and gives weight to the petition for help in the present distress. 

*Thou art my King, O God: command deliverances for Jacob.*

Thou art our Lord, command deliverance to thy people.

<!-- post 17923 · 2026-10-02 05:53 · study -->

While individual prayers can and should follow this pattern of recognition and thanksgiving of past actions, before requesting aid for the current troubles, this Psalm is specifically a communal prayer for God's people. Faithful Israel, the nation in the past, spiritual Israel today. 

Collectively, Christians have faced persecution and tribulations, by the enemies of Christ. 1st century persecution by the Jews and the Romans, the Dark Ages martyrdom of believers at the hands of the Roman Catholic Church. The mass murder by Muslims against Nigerian Christians today. 

The same will be suffered in the future, when the beast makes war on the saints during Tribulation.

American Christians today may posture and pose about their hypothetical steadfastness during a supposed tribulation in their lifetime. That they will gladly offer their necks to the sword for Jesus. Boasting of their own willingness to sacrifice themselves as martyrs for the promised crowns in the post-trib pre-wrath time to come. 

Or, like a vast majority of the brethren, believe fervently that they will actually be spared any persecution in the end times by being whisked away before it even starts. The pre-trib wishful thinking that dominates Christian churches today, leads to a false comfort that political solutions will forestall the inevitable that they've excused themselves from, rather than repentance and prayer, soul-winning and standards. 

But either group may look upon the persecuted saints today, or the congregation at the time of **Psalm 44**, and see their plaintive questions against God, and His seeming abandonment of His people, and look down our noses at it. 

"If it were us, in our day, we wouldn't blame God for allowing those that hate us to spoil for themselves! We wouldn't question why God has cast us off, and left us to die!" 

The same Christians who cry out in pain at every "Happy holidays!" and sit on their couches when there are doors to knock and souls to save. Who prioritize football and recreation over church. Who bemoan the wickedness of the world, while living exact like it.

<!-- post 17924 · 2026-10-02 06:26 · study -->

**1 Peter 4:12–14**
*Beloved, think it not strange concerning the fiery trial which is to try you, as though some strange thing happened unto you: But rejoice, inasmuch as ye are partakers of Christ’s sufferings; that, when his glory shall be revealed, ye may be glad also with exceeding joy. If ye be reproached for the name of Christ, happy are ye; for the spirit of glory and of God resteth upon you: on their part he is evil spoken of, but on your part he is glorified.*

If the people who call themselves by His name we're naturally like Stephen, standing up for the faith and being willing to suffer death for the truth while forgiving those that stoned him to death, the New Testament wouldn't be so full of warnings and encouragement. 

In **Psalm 44:22–24** they cry out desperately to God;
*Yea, for thy sake are we killed all the day long; we are counted as sheep for the slaughter. Awake, why sleepest thou, O Lord? arise, cast us not off for ever. Wherefore hidest thou thy face, and forgettest our affliction and our oppression?* 

The same lament is turned to edifying instruction in the New Testament;

**Romans 8:35–37**
*Who shall separate us from the love of Christ? shall tribulation, or distress, or persecution, or famine, or nakedness, or peril, or sword? As it is written, For thy sake we are killed all the day long; we are accounted as sheep for the slaughter. Nay, in all these things we are more than conquerors through him that loved us.*

Because man is naturally going to complain in the best of times, we need to be prepared for the proper attitude in the worst. To be persuaded that nothing can separate us from the love of Christ. Even when things are bleak. 

Jesus Himself gave this encouragement to the church in Smyrna, who were facing real persecution at the hands of their enemies. 

**Revelation 2:8–11**
*And unto the angel of the church in Smyrna write; These things saith the first and the last, which was dead, and is alive; I know thy works, and tribulation, and poverty, (but thou art rich) and I know the blasphemy of them which say they are Jews, and are not, but are the synagogue of Satan. Fear none of those things which thou shalt suffer: behold, the devil shall cast some of you into prison, that ye may be tried; and ye shall have tribulation ten days: be thou faithful unto death, and I will give thee a crown of life. He that hath an ear, let him hear what the Spirit saith unto the churches; He that overcometh shall not be hurt of the second death.*

Just as the instructions to live separated lives, to be holy as He is holy, is necessary because man does not by his fleshly nature do good works, even those who are saved, the same flesh tends to need reminders of past victory, while still may cry out "Why?" to their Saviour. 

Righteous Job did. The best man of his generation, and yet he sought answers from God, and sinned not with his mouth while doing so, according to scripture. 

Modern American Christians, made weak by the comforts and bounty God has graciously given our nation, grown fat off His blessings, will most assuredly cry out the same laments when the abomination of desolation is rolled out, and the armies of the beast strike us down. 

Even the spirits of the martyrs in Great Tribulation will cry out, impatiently.

**Revelation 6:9–10**
*And when he had opened the fifth seal, I saw under the altar the souls of them that were slain for the word of God, and for the testimony which they held: And they cried with a loud voice, saying, How long, O Lord, holy and true, dost thou not judge and avenge our blood on them that dwell on the earth?*

We naturally may feel abandoned in the thick of it. Crying out, as the congregation does, is sadly expected. 

*Awake, why sleepest thou, O Lord? arise, cast us not off for ever. Wherefore hidest thou thy face, and forgettest our affliction and our oppression?*

We may be beaten down, bowed to the dust, despondent.

<!-- post 17925 · 2026-10-02 06:26 · study -->

But remember the stories told by our fathers. Remember the victories God has given us. Remember that we have already overcome in Christ. That nothing will separate us from His love. 

We may turn to the Lord in fear. We may be consumed by our enemies, and feel forgotten. We may be impatient in the face of the tribulation. 

But the key is to turn towards God in our prayers. 

*Arise for our help, and redeem us for thy mercies’ sake.* Amen.

<!-- post 17926 · 2026-10-02 06:50 · study -->

What makes **Psalm 44** so jarring and often makes modern readers uncomfortable, is that the congregation is stating they are not being punished for apostasy or adultery or for being unfaithful. 

In many other laments, Israel confesses their sins and acknowledges that their defeat is a righteous judgment from God. But here, they boldly declare their innocence: 

*All this is come upon us; yet have we not forgotten thee, neither have we dealt falsely in thy covenant... If we have forgotten the name of our God... Shall not God search this out?*

Travails come upon the just. Upon the faithful who have placed their trust in the Lord. 

The psalmists raise a relevant point today; believers may cry out to God and ask why they are suffering, knowing they have not fallen to apostasy. 

These sons of Korah are believers, and believers cannot apostatize. They feel confident in asking God to search their hearts for any sign of adultery in worship of Him. 

They may, amongst themselves, talk about how the northern kingdom of Israel which was also being afflicted in this time by the Assyrian empire, deserved it. They had fallen into the sin of Jeroboam and gone after strange gods. 

But these faithful Levites could wonder what they themselves may have done to bring upon these attacks. 

Whatever other sins they may have had in their lives or collectively, they still were faithful to the Lord. 

They did exactly what Deuteronomy 6 commanded. They listened to their fathers. They trusted not in their own bows or swords. They kept the faith, yet they were still *scattered among the heathen* and *counted as sheep for the slaughter*, and yet still faced tribulation. 

Paul quotes **Psalm 44** in **Romans** for exactly this reason. To show that God eventually does deliver the faithful. That we can count on him even when it seems like the entire world is against us. 

Paul is using the same technique of teaching to the children the stories of the fathers, to bring us hope. Judah was delivered from the Assyrians. And the faithful were delivered by the promise of Christ being fulfilled. 

Using then a past lament to give future hope, and show the strength of God in keeping His promises is the entire point of the commandment. 

We see the Deliverance of God's people into the promised land in the past used as hope for the people then present Jerusalem, and their cries are then given to us as hope today knowing that they were eventually delivered. 

If we are scattered to the wilderness, to the place of dragons, or are led through the valley of the shadow of death, we have that hope within us. The call to have the patience of Job. The faith of the fathers. The understanding of Paul. 

So stand fast and comfort one another with these words.

## Psalms 45  ·  Primary study  ·  2026-10-03

Source: https://t.me/HouseOfWews2/17931

<!-- post 17931 · 2026-10-03 06:30 · scripture -->

**Psalms 45**
*My heart is inditing a good matter: I speak of the things which I have made touching the king: my tongue is the pen of a ready writer.*

*2 Thou art fairer than the children of men: grace is poured into thy lips: therefore God hath blessed thee for ever.*

*3 Gird thy sword upon thy thigh, O most mighty, with thy glory and thy majesty.*

*4 And in thy majesty ride prosperously because of truth and meekness and righteousness; and thy right hand shall teach thee terrible things.*

*5 Thine arrows are sharp in the heart of the king’s enemies; whereby the people fall under thee.*

*6 Thy throne, O God, is for ever and ever: the sceptre of thy kingdom is a right sceptre.*

*7 Thou lovest righteousness, and hatest wickedness: therefore God, thy God, hath anointed thee with the oil of gladness above thy fellows.*

*8 All thy garments smell of myrrh, and aloes, and cassia, out of the ivory palaces, whereby they have made thee glad.*

*9 Kings’ daughters were among thy honourable women: upon thy right hand did stand the queen in gold of Ophir.*

*10 Hearken, O daughter, and consider, and incline thine ear; forget also thine own people, and thy father’s house;*

*11 So shall the king greatly desire thy beauty: for he is thy Lord; and worship thou him.*

*12 And the daughter of Tyre shall be there with a gift; even the rich among the people shall intreat thy favour.*

*13 The king’s daughter is all glorious within: her clothing is of wrought gold.*

*14 She shall be brought unto the king in raiment of needlework: the virgins her companions that follow her shall be brought unto thee.*

*15 With gladness and rejoicing shall they be brought: they shall enter into the king’s palace.*

*16 Instead of thy fathers shall be thy children, whom thou mayest make princes in all the earth.*

*17 I will make thy name to be remembered in all generations: therefore shall the people praise thee for ever and ever.*

<!-- post 17932 · 2026-10-03 07:20 · study -->

**Psalm 45** is a doctrine-rich wedding song, that speaks clearly of the Lord Jesus, a bride brought to Him, and the promise of exaltation for the King of Kings. 

Of course, the dual nature is both a historic royal wedding song and a profound Messianic prophecy, the Psalms often capture both layers in a way that makes them relevant to our lives as believers. It is a testimony to their divine inspiration. 

The same inspiration described in the introduction found in verse 1. 

The author says his heart is inditing a good matter, meaning fit to burst with the understanding he is speaking of. His *tongue is the pen of a ready writer.* 

God has given him the idea, the words, His inspired, God breathed need to speak and record the word of God, that is bubbling our of his heart. 

Jesus said *out of the abundance of the heart the mouth speaketh*, be it the evil of the Pharisees or the inspired word. Whether the penman, or those who have committed the word to our hearts. 

**2 Peter 1:21**
*For the prophecy came not in old time by the will of man: but holy men of God spake as they were moved by the Holy Ghost.*

And so, moved by the Holy Ghost, given the word of God, the ready writer gives us a song of romance and prophecy. Deeply layered and rich, as only scripture can be. 

A similar song to Solomon's, messianic prophecy couched in a romantic allusion. 

Verse 2 begins the description of One, fairer than all men, full of grace, blessed forever by God. The Christ. His chosen. The groom, and King. 

Girded with His sword, the most mighty. Almighty. A name of God given this person. All glory and majesty are His. 

Riding majestically, and prosperously, upon a horse to battle. 

**Revelation 19:11-13**
*And I saw heaven opened, and behold a white horse; and he that sat upon him was called Faithful and True, and in righteousness he doth judge and make war. His eyes were as a flame of fire, and on his head were many crowns; and he had a name written, that no man knew, but he himself. And he was clothed with a vesture dipped in blood: and his name is called The Word of God*.

He has the word of God proceeding from His lips. Destroying the enemies of God with His sharp sword. The strength of His right hand, teaching *thee terrible things*. 

What a picture that's painted. What a powerful, Almighty Son of God. Who comes to smite the enemies of His Father, and rule with a rod of iron, but His prosperity is in His truth and meekness and righteousness. 

The terrible things, are in this context, the fearful lessons of the Lord. And these are grounded in those three qualities. The truth of the word, the meekness of our Savior, His righteousness in all things. 

For the Christian, these three qualities are what we should pursue. We should gird ourselves with the sword of Truth, knowing His word in our hearts, ready to deliver it. 

**Hebrews 4:12**
*For the word of God is quick, and powerful, and sharper than any twoedged sword, piercing even to the dividing asunder of soul and spirit, and of the joints and marrow, and is a discerner of the thoughts and intents of the heart.*

Living in meekness, a servant. Condescending to men of low estate, that they may be saved. Putting our heart to The afflicted, the widow, the fatherless, the poor. For the meek shall inherit the Earth. 

And living in righteousness. Doing right things by the judgment of God found in His word. Cleansed by the blood and carrying the righteousness of Christ within us. Being holy, as He is holy. 

*Thou lovest righteousness, and hatest wickedness: therefore God, thy God, hath anointed thee with the oil of gladness above thy fellows.*

Truth, meekness and righteousness means loving what is good and hating wickedness. This is the inheritance of the Lord and the perfection of it found in Christ Jesus. 

**Hebrews 1:8–9**
*But unto the Son he saith, Thy throne, O God, is for ever and ever: a sceptre of righteousness is the sceptre of thy kingdom. Thou hast loved righteousness, and hated iniquity; therefore God, even thy God, hath anointed thee with the oil of gladness above thy fellows.*

<!-- post 17933 · 2026-10-03 07:20 · study -->

Christ, the Anointed One of God.

<!-- post 17934 · 2026-10-03 08:22 · study -->

After establishing His love for righteousness, the text states that He is anointed with the *oil of gladness* and that His garments smell of myrrh, aloes, and cassia. 

As messianic prophecy, the elements of His anointment listed are symbols of Jesus, His death and His triumph over death. 

The oil of gladness, olive oil, used to anoint kings and in celebration. To freshen the face, and to signify joy. 

We see throughout scripture that oil is used as a metaphor for the Spirit. And Jesus, at the start of His ministry is anointed by the Spirit from the Father, showing to John and the other witnesses that Jesus was the Christ. 

**Matthew 3:16-17** 
*And Jesus, when he was baptized, went up straightway out of the water: and, lo, the heavens were opened unto him, and he saw the Spirit of God descending like a dove, and lighting upon him: And lo a voice from heaven, saying, This is my beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased.*

While earthly kings were anointed with physical oil, Jesus was anointed directly by the Holy Spirit without measure.

**John 3:33–35**
*He that hath received his testimony hath set to his seal that God is true. For he whom God hath sent speaketh the words of God: for God giveth not the Spirit by measure unto him. The Father loveth the Son, and hath given all things into his hand.*

And so, the ultimate joy, the true anointing Is given to Jesus the Christ. 

**Philippians 2:8–10**
*And being found in fashion as a man, he humbled himself, and became obedient unto death, even the death of the cross. Wherefore God also hath highly exalted him, and given him a name which is above every name: That at the name of Jesus every knee should bow, of things in heaven, and things in earth, and things under the earth*

The meekness of the sinless Son of God, going to the cross, despising the shame to be raised into glory, as the King of righteousness. The truth by which we are set free. 

**Isaiah 61** says that He that was anointed by the Spirit of God to *preach good tidings to the meek..bind up the brokenhearted...proclaim liberty to the captives,* and to give unto them that trust in that truth..*.the oil of joy for mourning, the garment of praise for the spirit of heaviness; that they might be called trees of righteousness, the planting of the LORD, that he might be glorified.*

The Messiah, the Anointed One, the Christ gives us the anointing of the Spirit by faith, that we may be His fruit, the trees of righteousness, planted that He might be glorified.

<!-- post 17935 · 2026-10-03 08:39 · study -->

This is why, so frequently in the Gospels and Epistles, Christ is used as a kind of shorthand that carries all of this meaning in the context of Jesus being that Anointed One. 

**John 1:40–42**
*One of the two which heard John speak, and followed him, was Andrew, Simon Peter’s brother. He first findeth his own brother Simon, and saith unto him, We have found the Messias, which is, being interpreted, the Christ. And he brought him to Jesus. And when Jesus beheld him, he said, Thou art Simon the son of Jona: thou shalt be called Cephas, which is by interpretation, A stone.*

Andrew heard from John the Baptist, that Jesus was the Messias, the Christ, and immediately knew what that meant. He tells Simon Peter, who understands the implications. 

The term "Christ" was a heavily loaded, shorthand title that required no basic definition for a 1st-century, faithful, Jewish audience. 

The concept of a coming, anointed deliverer was deeply woven into the fabric of the Jews' expectation, rooted in Old Testament prophecies of a King from the line of David, a Priest, or a Prophet, to deliver them. 

In **Matthew 16:16**, Peter delivers his landmark declaration: *Thou art the Christ, the Son of the living God.*

During Jesus' trial, the high priest asks him point-blank: 

**Mark 14:60–61**
*And the high priest stood up in the midst, and asked Jesus, saying, Answerest thou nothing? what is it which these witness against thee? But he held his peace, and answered nothing. Again the high priest asked him, and said unto him, Art thou the Christ, the Son of the Blessed?*

The religious leaders had an understanding of the scriptural definition of the Christ; they just believed Jesus was blasphemously claiming it.

But it is His to claim. And faith in this truth is what is required of man to be saved. The shorthand of the Christ, that carries so much spiritual weight.

**1 John 5:1**
*Whosoever believeth that Jesus is the Christ is born of God: and every one that loveth him that begat loveth him also that is begotten of him*.

And His anointing of joy is then given to we who believe, that our joy always be full.

**1 John 1:3–4**
*That which we have seen and heard declare we unto you, that ye also may have fellowship with us: and truly our fellowship is with the Father, and with his Son Jesus Christ. And these things write we unto you, that your joy may be full*.

<!-- post 17936 · 2026-10-03 09:03 · study -->

The anointing oil is based on olive oil, but contains the other spices for fragrance, myrrh and cassia.

**Exodus 30:22–29**
*Moreover the LORD spake unto Moses, saying, Take thou also unto thee principal spices, of pure myrrh five hundred shekels, and of sweet cinnamon half so much, even two hundred and fifty shekels, and of sweet calamus two hundred and fifty shekels, And of cassia five hundred shekels, after the shekel of the sanctuary, and of oil olive an hin: And thou shalt make it an oil of holy ointment, an ointment compound after the art of the apothecary: it shall be an holy anointing oil. And thou shalt anoint the tabernacle of the congregation therewith, and the ark of the testimony, And the table and all his vessels, and the candlestick and his vessels, and the altar of incense, And the altar of burnt offering with all his vessels, and the laver and his foot. And thou shalt sanctify them, that they may be most holy: whatsoever toucheth them shall be holy.*

This ointment is given of God to sanctify, and make all that it is applied to, holy. But it was reserved for the items of the Tabernacle and the High Priest, a holy anointing oil.

**Exodus 30:32**
*Upon man’s flesh shall it not be poured, neither shall ye make any other like it, after the composition of it: it is holy, and it shall be holy unto you.*

God also instructed not to anoint a stranger with this, as it is holy, threatening to cut off any who do it from the people. 

Yet it is this holy oil, reserved for the things of God and His High Priest, that is given to the King described in **Psalm 45**.

Myrrh is a bitter resin extracted from a thorny tree, that was primarily used in burial preparations. It was given as a gift for a King by the wisemen visiting the infant Jesus. 

Nicodemus used myrrh and aloes in the preparation of the body of Christ at His burial. 

**John 19:39–40**
*And there came also Nicodemus, which at the first came to Jesus by night, and brought a mixture of myrrh and aloes, about an hundred pound weight. Then took they the body of Jesus, and wound it in linen clothes with the spices, as the manner of the Jews is to bury.*

There is a sense of the suffering of Christ, the death He tasted for all men, in the application of myrrh in the Psalm, reflected in the Gospels. 

The scent of myrrh on the King carries with it His majesty, that cannot be separated from His sacrificial death. His garments carry the scent of His ultimate act of love. 

The aloes mentioned are not the oil we know today, but the fragrance made from an eaglewood tree, and those aloes are found in Nicodemus' mixture. Because aloes were used to preserve bodies and counteract the smell of decay, they symbolize resurrection, healing, and incorruptibility of Christ, in His death. 

**Psalms 16:10**
*For thou wilt not leave my soul in hell; neither wilt thou suffer thine Holy One to see corruption.*

Cassia is an aromatic bark, like cinnamon, and is used in the holy anointing oil, mentioned above. This ties in to the understanding of Christ not only as King but High Priest. 

Taken together, these three spices combined on the garments of the King paint a complete picture of the Gospel narrative. Myrrh as the sacrificial death, aloes His incorruption and resurrection, and cassia, Jesus' ascension, and role as High Priest, forever. 

And so in **Psalm 45**, as the King approaches His bride, He carries with Him the literal aroma of redemption. The scent of His gospel. Amen

<!-- post 17946 · 2026-10-06 06:04 · study -->

Continuing in **Psalm 45**, the messianic wedding song, we transition from the King to the bride. 

At His right hand stands the queen, decked in gold from Ophir, (a site somewhere south-southwest of Jerusalem renown for its gold).

Her ornamental beauty, provided by the King, to set apart the bride. The honorable women, the church. 

As a picture of believers, we see the Kings great desire for the beauty He has bestowed. He is truly Lord and we worship Him. 

*Hearken, O daughter, and consider, and incline thine ear; forget also thine own people, and thy father’s house*

We are called to hear the appeal from Heaven, to consider and incline our ear (*faith cometh by hearing*), to forget our people and our parents, and to become His. 

**Luke 14:26–27**
*If any man come to me, and hate not his father, and mother, and wife, and children, and brethren, and sisters, yea, and his own life also, he cannot be my disciple. And whosoever doth not bear his cross, and come after me, cannot be my disciple.*

We are called out, to be His above our family or national bonds. To "hate" here doesn't mean to dislike intensely, as it does today. It means to not love as much, to not prefer over another. To love Christ more than even your kin. 

**Matthew 10:35–37**
*For I am come to set a man at variance against his father, and the daughter against her mother, and the daughter in law against her mother in law. And a man’s foes shall be they of his own household. He that loveth father or mother more than me is not worthy of me: and he that loveth son or daughter more than me is not worthy of me.*

Just as a wife is called to separate from her parents and cleave unto the husband. 

**Genesis 2:24**
*Therefore shall a man leave his father and his mother, and shall cleave unto his wife: and they shall be one flesh.*

Becoming the bride of Christ, cleaving unto Him, placing our love for Him above others. A wedding is a picture of this relationship that we have. It is given as a parable, that we may better know Him. 

In our status as His, the great of every nation shall show us favor. Perhaps in the Millennial Kingdom, certainly not in this life. But when we have come into the inheritance, they will respect our rule because Jesus is their Lord, and we are His. 

We are arrayed as a burden in gold and fine embroidery, the white garments of purity, the righteousness of Christ that covers us. 

*With gladness and rejoicing shall they be brought: they shall enter into the king’s palace*

**Luke 15:10**
*Likewise, I say unto you, there is joy in the presence of the angels of God over one sinner that repenteth.*

This is the celebration of salvation. And the future wedding supper we are called to. 

*Instead of thy fathers shall be thy children, whom thou mayest make princes in all the earth.*

We will not look to our earthly heritage, the genealogies and lineage, but instead at those we have won to the Gospel, as children who rule and reign with Christ. It is our godly heritage, the spiritual children we present to Christ. 

Looking forward to the future of those we have helped come to repentance. This is our vow to our Lord and Savior. 

*I will make thy name to be remembered in all generations: therefore shall the people praise thee for ever and ever*. Amen

## Psalms 46  ·  Primary study  ·  2026-10-07

Source: https://t.me/HouseOfWews2/17948

<!-- post 17948 · 2026-10-07 05:46 · scripture -->

**Psalms 46**
*God is our refuge and strength, a very present help in trouble.*

*2 Therefore will not we fear, though the earth be removed, and though the mountains be carried into the midst of the sea;*

*3 Though the waters thereof roar and be troubled, though the mountains shake with the swelling thereof. Selah.*

*4 There is a river, the streams whereof shall make glad the city of God, the holy place of the tabernacles of the most High.*

*5 God is in the midst of her; she shall not be moved: God shall help her, and that right early.*

*6 The heathen raged, the kingdoms were moved: he uttered his voice, the earth melted.*

*7 The LORD of hosts is with us; the God of Jacob is our refuge. Selah.*

*8 Come, behold the works of the LORD, what desolations he hath made in the earth.*

*9 He maketh wars to cease unto the end of the earth; he breaketh the bow, and cutteth the spear in sunder; he burneth the chariot in the fire.*

*10 Be still, and know that I am God: I will be exalted among the heathen, I will be exalted in the earth.*

*11 The LORD of hosts is with us; the God of Jacob is our refuge. Selah.*

<!-- post 17949 · 2026-10-07 06:03 · study -->

The opening verse of **Psalm 46** is a wonderful text on the faithfulness and protection of God. It has been preached on an untold number of times, been made a life-verse for scores of people, been pinned to walls, stuck on refrigerators, is a prominent part of hundreds of thousands of devotionals, verse of the days, and used in countless facebook memes. 

What a comfort for believers in trouble these words have been and continue to be. 

Again and again, the Bible tells us that God is our refuge and strength. Our buckler, our fortress, our shield and strong tower. The promise of protection extended to the children of God is found throughout the word, and ultimately the entire Bible is a promise of deliverance from a holy and merciful God. 

The Gospel itself is the promise of protection and life, from God to all who come to Him. And the promise is kept in the Son, the Lord Christ Jesus. 

He is our rock. He is our strong foundation. He is the gathering hen, protecting her chicks under her wing. He is the Good Shepherd, watching over the flock, His love for His sheep so deep, that He laid down His life for us. 

God is very present, always with us, desiring to be our Comfort, to be involved in our lives, and our shelter in the time of storm. 

And in any Christian life, given enough time, this is proven to us again and again.

<!-- post 17950 · 2026-10-07 06:10 · study -->

**Psalm 46:1**
*God is our refuge and strength, a very present help in trouble.*

**Psalm 91:2**
*I will say of the Lord, He is my refuge and my fortress: my God; in him will I trust.*

**Proverbs 18:10**
*The name of the Lord is a strong tower: the righteous runneth into it, and is safe.*

**Psalm 18:2**
*The Lord is my rock, and my fortress, and my deliverer; my God, my strength, in whom I will trust; my buckler, and the horn of my salvation, and my high tower.*

**Psalm 121:7**
*The Lord shall preserve thee from all evil: he shall preserve thy soul.*

**Deuteronomy 31:6**
*Be strong and of a good courage, fear not, nor be afraid of them: for the Lord thy God, he it is that doth go with thee; he will not fail thee, nor forsake thee.*

**2 Thessalonians 3:3**
*But the Lord is faithful, who shall stablish you, and keep you from evil.*

**Isaiah 41:10**
*Fear thou not; for I am with thee: be not dismayed; for I am thy God: I will strengthen thee; yea, I will help thee; yea, I will uphold thee with the right hand of my righteousness*.

**Psalm 34:17**
*The righteous cry, and the Lord heareth, and delivereth them out of all their troubles.*

**Nahum 1:7**
*The Lord is good, a strong hold in the day of trouble; and he knoweth them that trust in him.*

These are not platitudes or empty promises. These are real and observable and heartfelt understandings and expressions of faith, forged in troubled times. 

God so loved the world that He gave His only begotten Son to keep this promise. God commanded His love towards us that while we were yet sinners, Christ died for us.

We know, by the testifying of the Holy Spirit inside of us, that Spirit of adoption, that we are the Father's and His children are protected.

**Psalm 47** is a song of praise for the Lord, the King of kings. Christ Jesus, Who shall put every nation under His feet. 

**Revelation 11:15–17**
*And the seventh angel sounded; and there were great voices in heaven, saying, The kingdoms of this world are become the kingdoms of our Lord, and of his Christ; and he shall reign for ever and ever. And the four and twenty elders, which sat before God on their seats, fell upon their faces, and worshipped God, Saying, We give thee thanks, O LORD God Almighty, which art, and wast, and art to come; because thou hast taken to thee thy great power, and hast reigned.*

He shall rule and reign with a rod of iron, and give to all His people their inheritance in His Kingdom. 

Jesus is God. Verse 5 says *God is gone up with a shout, the LORD with the sound of a trumpet.* A prophetic Old Testament verse, made clear in the New Testament, carrying the comfort of all believers;

**1 Thessalonians 4:16–17**
*For the Lord himself shall descend from heaven with a shout, with the voice of the archangel, and with the trump of God: and the dead in Christ shall rise first: Then we which are alive and remain shall be caught up together with them in the clouds, to meet the Lord in the air: and so shall we ever be with the Lord.*

We shall sing of the glories of the Lord, forever. 

**Revelation 7:10–12**
*And cried with a loud voice, saying, Salvation to our God which sitteth upon the throne, and unto the Lamb. And all the angels stood round about the throne, and about the elders and the four beasts, and fell before the throne on their faces, and worshipped God, Saying, Amen: Blessing, and glory, and wisdom, and thanksgiving, and honour, and power, and might, be unto our God for ever and ever. Amen.*

**Revelation 19:16**
*And he hath on his vesture and on his thigh a name written, KING OF KINGS, AND LORD OF LORDS.*

For Jesus is the King of all the earth, and reigns over the heathen. All the nations. He is greatly exalted above every name. 

**Philippians 2:9–11**
*Wherefore God also hath highly exalted him, and given him a name which is above every name: That at the name of Jesus every knee should bow, of things in heaven, and things in earth, and things under the earth; And that every tongue should confess that Jesus Christ is Lord, to the glory of God the Father.*

Jesus will reign over all things, and, having defeated death and been made ruler of all, He will give all to God the Father. A perfect sacrifice, holy and acceptable, made holy by His righteousness:

**1 Corinthians 15:24–27**
*Then cometh the end, when he shall have delivered up the kingdom to God, even the Father; when he shall have put down all rule and all authority and power. For he must reign, till he hath put all enemies under his feet. The last enemy that shall be destroyed is death. For he hath put all things under his feet. But when he saith all things are put under him, it is manifest that he is excepted, which did put all things under him*.

**Psalm 47** is a wonderful and prophetic song of praise and worship for the Kingdom of God, under our Lord Christ Jesus. All honor and praise is His. Amen

## Psalms 48  ·  Primary study  ·  2026-10-14

Source: https://t.me/HouseOfWews2/17968

<!-- post 17968 · 2026-10-14 05:40 · scripture -->

**Psalms 48**
*Great is the LORD, and greatly to be praised in the city of our God, in the mountain of his holiness.*

*2 Beautiful for situation, the joy of the whole earth, is mount Zion, on the sides of the north, the city of the great King.*

*3 God is known in her palaces for a refuge.*

*4 For, lo, the kings were assembled, they passed by together.*

*5 They saw it, and so they marvelled; they were troubled, and hasted away.*

*6 Fear took hold upon them there, and pain, as of a woman in travail.*

*7 Thou breakest the ships of Tarshish with an east wind.*

*8 As we have heard, so have we seen in the city of the LORD of hosts, in the city of our God: God will establish it for ever. Selah.*

*9 We have thought of thy lovingkindness, O God, in the midst of thy temple.*

*10 According to thy name, O God, so is thy praise unto the ends of the earth: thy right hand is full of righteousness.*

*11 Let mount Zion rejoice, let the daughters of Judah be glad, because of thy judgments.*

*12 Walk about Zion, and go round about her: tell the towers thereof.*

*13 Mark ye well her bulwarks, consider her palaces; that ye may tell it to the generation following.*

*14 For this God is our God for ever and ever: he will be our guide even unto death.*

<!-- post 17969 · 2026-10-14 06:12 · study -->

Mount Zion, Jerusalem, is often referenced and praised as the place God chose to place His name. His holy city, the mountain providing natural protection on 3 sides, situated perfectly. A river running through the fertile valley. The setting where Abraham brought Issac. Where God prepared Himself a sacrifice. Where Jesus was slain and rose again, and where He will reign in the Kingdom to come. 

The name, Zion, means a “monument; raised up; sepulcher,” the place of the tomb, hewn out of rock, the site of Christ's burial. Chosen by God. 

Jerusalem, the city isn't a lonely peak, but rather a settlement nestled within a protective bowl of higher terrain, with Mt Moriah and the mount of Olives part of the limestone rings, defending the area. 

**Psalm 125:1–2**
*They that trust in the LORD shall be as mount Zion, which cannot be removed, but abideth for ever. As the mountains are round about Jerusalem, so the LORD is round about his people from henceforth even for ever.*

Beautiful for situation, a perfect location, the city if the great King on its north face. 

**Psalm 132:13–14**
*For the LORD hath chosen Zion; he hath desired it for his habitation. This is my rest for ever: here will I dwell; for I have desired it.*

**1 Peter 2:5–6**
*Ye also, as lively stones, are built up a spiritual house, an holy priesthood, to offer up spiritual sacrifices, acceptable to God by Jesus Christ. Wherefore also it is contained in the scripture, Behold, I lay in Sion a chief corner stone, elect, precious: and he that believeth on him shall not be confounded.*

The shelter of Zion, the cornerstone of which is Christ, is where Jesus returns to in the end times, with the 144,000 witnesses. 

**Revelation 14:1**
*And I looked, and, lo, a Lamb stood on the mount Sion, and with him an hundred forty and four thousand, having his Father’s name written in their foreheads.*

The defenses of this holy mountain were enough to dissuade kings and armies. 

Sennacherib, of Assyria, the most powerful emperor of the ancient world, invaded Judah with a massive army of 185,000 soldiers. 

He sent his top military commanders to the walls of Jerusalem, fiercely mocking King Hezekiah and blaspheming God, confidently expecting the city to fall just like every other nation before it. 

Hezekiah called out to the Lord for protection of the city. 

**2 Kings 19:16–19**
*LORD, bow down thine ear, and hear: open, LORD, thine eyes, and see: and hear the words of Sennacherib, which hath sent him to reproach the living God. Of a truth, LORD, the kings of Assyria have destroyed the nations and their lands, And have cast their gods into the fire: for they were no gods, but the work of men’s hands, wood and stone: therefore they have destroyed them. Now therefore, O LORD our God, I beseech thee, save thou us out of his hand, that all the kingdoms of the earth may know that thou art the LORD God, even thou only.*

And God harkened to the prayer, for His own purposes and will;

**2 Kings 19:32–34**
*Therefore thus saith the LORD concerning the king of Assyria, He shall not come into this city, nor shoot an arrow there, nor come before it with shield, nor cast a bank against it. By the way that he came, by the same shall he return, and shall not come into this city, saith the LORD. For I will defend this city, to save it, for mine own sake, and for my servant David’s sake.*

Zion is defended for His own sake, because He placed His name there, and for the promise made to David that out of Zion shall come a King. 

**Romans 11:26–27**
*And so all Israel shall be saved: as it is written, There shall come out of Sion the Deliverer, and shall turn away ungodliness from Jacob: For this is my covenant unto them, when I shall take away their sins.*

Zion is holy, because it has been separated unto the purposes of God. 

It is not holy because the people there are holy. God allowed Babylon to raze the city and remove the people. Jesus decreed that Jerusalem would fall, and in 70AD it did. With the end times approaching, the city is considered wicked;

<!-- post 17970 · 2026-10-14 06:12 · study -->

**Revelation 11:8.**
*And their dead bodies shall lie in the street of the great city, which spiritually is called Sodom and Egypt, where also our Lord was crucified.*

As Christ said;

**Matthew 23:37–38**
*O Jerusalem, Jerusalem, thou that killest the prophets, and stonest them which are sent unto thee, how often would I have gathered thy children together, even as a hen gathereth her chickens under her wings, and ye would not! Behold, your house is left unto you desolate.*

The Jews cursed the land, building up Zion with blood of the righteous;

**Micah 3:9–10**
*Hear this, I pray you, ye heads of the house of Jacob, and princes of the house of Israel, that abhor judgment, and pervert all equity. They build up Zion with blood, and Jerusalem with iniquity.*

**Lamentations 4:11–13**
*The LORD hath accomplished his fury; he hath poured out his fierce anger, and hath kindled a fire in Zion, and it hath devoured the foundations thereof. The kings of the earth, and all the inhabitants of the world, would not have believed that the adversary and the enemy should have entered into the gates of Jerusalem. For the sins of her prophets, and the iniquities of her priests, that have shed the blood of the just in the midst of her*

<!-- post 17971 · 2026-10-14 06:39 · study -->

But, Zion, like believers, stained crimson in sin, has a redeemer. And, like His church, Jerusalem is His bride, made perfect and decked in the finest array, fit for the King. 

**Isaiah 61:10**
*I will greatly rejoice in the Lord, my soul shall be joyful in my God; for he hath clothed me with the garments of salvation, he hath covered me with the robe of righteousness, as a bridegroom decketh himself with ornaments, and as a bride adorneth herself with her jewels.*

**Isaiah 62:1–7**
*For Zion’s sake will I not hold my peace, and for Jerusalem’s sake I will not rest, until the righteousness thereof go forth as brightness, and the salvation thereof as a lamp that burneth. And the Gentiles shall see thy righteousness, and all kings thy glory: and thou shalt be called by a new name, which the mouth of the LORD shall name. Thou shalt also be a crown of glory in the hand of the LORD, and a royal diadem in the hand of thy God. Thou shalt no more be termed Forsaken; neither shall thy land any more be termed Desolate: but thou shalt be called Hephzibah, and thy land Beulah: for the LORD delighteth in thee, and thy land shall be married. For as a young man marrieth a virgin, so shall thy sons marry thee: and as the bridegroom rejoiceth over the bride, so shall thy God rejoice over thee. I have set watchmen upon thy walls, O Jerusalem, which shall never hold their peace day nor night: ye that make mention of the LORD, keep not silence, And give him no rest, till he establish, and till he make Jerusalem a praise in the earth.*

Zion, and Jerusalem, are a picture of the believer. A bride of Christ, made precious by His redemption, protecting it like a jealous husband; 

**Zechariah 1:14**
*So the angel that communed with me said unto me, Cry thou, saying, Thus saith the Lord of hosts; I am jealous for Jerusalem and for Zion with a great jealousy.*

**Isaiah 54:5–7**
*For thy Maker is thine husband; the LORD of hosts is his name; and thy Redeemer the Holy One of Israel; The God of the whole earth shall he be called. For the LORD hath called thee as a woman forsaken and grieved in spirit, and a wife of youth, when thou wast refused, saith thy God. For a small moment have I forsaken thee; but with great mercies will I gather thee.*

In the end, we shall see New Jerusalem, upon his holy hill, Zion. Perfected as a picture of his people, His bride;

**Revelation 21:1–2**
*And I saw a new heaven and a new earth: for the first heaven and the first earth were passed away; and there was no more sea. And I John saw the holy city, new Jerusalem, coming down from God out of heaven, prepared as a bride adorned for her husband.*

This is the promise of his holy place, how much more so is it the promise for those who have trusted in Christ. In an unmovable Rock, the cornerstone laid in Zion. The Rock of our salvation, we who are the bride of Christ. Amen

<!-- post 17974 · 2026-10-15 06:08 · study -->

**Psalm 48** broadly captures the grandeur and love of God towards His holy land. This love is seen long before Israel as a nation enters in, or even exists. It was in the beginning, when God promised a land to Abraham. 

**Genesis 12:1–3**
*Now the LORD had said unto Abram, Get thee out of thy country, and from thy kindred, and from thy father’s house, unto a land that I will shew thee: And I will make of thee a great nation, and I will bless thee, and make thy name great; and thou shalt be a blessing: And I will bless them that bless thee, and curse him that curseth thee: and in thee shall all families of the earth be blessed.*

The promise to Abraham was both for the land, and the Christ. Long before the exodus. Long before the wandering. Before Isaac was ever born, the land that would become Israel, the province of Judah, the city of Jerusalem, in the mountains of Zion, was set apart for God. 

The blessings and inheritance made to Abraham, who believed God and it was counted for righteousness, was made to him and his seed. Jesus Christ. 

**Galatians 3:16**
*Now to Abraham and his seed were the promises made. He saith not, And to seeds, as of many; but as of one, And to thy seed, which is Christ.*

Zionism is a modern political ideology that takes the promise to Abraham, and rather than give the inheritance and blessing to Christ, and all who are in Christ, gives it to unbelieving, apostate Jews. 

**Galatians 3:27–29**
*For as many of you as have been baptized into Christ have put on Christ. There is neither Jew nor Greek, there is neither bond nor free, there is neither male nor female: for ye are all one in Christ Jesus. And if ye be Christ’s, then are ye Abraham’s seed, and heirs according to the promise.*

The claim for the children of Israel to the land, by promise of God, was fulfilled in the time of Joshua. 

**Joshua 21:43–45**
*And the LORD gave unto Israel all the land which he sware to give unto their fathers; and they possessed it, and dwelt therein. And the LORD gave them rest round about, according to all that he sware unto their fathers: and there stood not a man of all their enemies before them; the LORD delivered all their enemies into their hand. There failed not ought of any good thing which the LORD had spoken unto the house of Israel; all came to pass.*

The promise to Abraham was unconditional. But the promise made to Moses by God in the wilderness, for His people to occupy His land, was conditional. 

**Deuteronomy 28:63–64**
*And it shall come to pass, that as the LORD rejoiced over you to do you good, and to multiply you; so the LORD will rejoice over you to destroy you, and to bring you to nought; and ye shall be plucked from off the land whither thou goest to possess it. And the LORD shall scatter thee among all people, from the one end of the earth even unto the other; and there thou shalt serve other gods, which neither thou nor thy fathers have known, even wood and stone.*

Many Christians today will say "God keeps His promises! The Jews deserve this land!" They place political Zionism and the ethnic ultranationalism of the modern Jewish state as being part of an unconditional and irrevocable covenant with the people by heritage and genealogy. 

But the Bible is clear, that the covenant made in the wilderness was explicitly conditional, that what was given to the people through Moses could not, by their failure, disannul the promise made to Abraham and his singular seed. 

**Galatians 3:17–18**
*And this I say, that the covenant, that was confirmed before of God in Christ, the law, which was four hundred and thirty years after, cannot disannul, that it should make the promise of none effect. For if the inheritance be of the law, it is no more of promise: but God gave it to Abraham by promise.*

But the covenant made those 430 years after Abraham? It has faded away, replace by a better covenant with better promises, to all who are in Christ, regardless of their ethnicity or origin.

<!-- post 17975 · 2026-10-15 06:08 · study -->

**Hebrews 8:7–10**
*For if that first covenant had been faultless, then should no place have been sought for the second. For finding fault with them, he saith, Behold, the days come, saith the Lord, when I will make a new covenant with the house of Israel and with the house of Judah: Not according to the covenant that I made with their fathers in the day when I took them by the hand to lead them out of the land of Egypt; because they continued not in my covenant, and I regarded them not, saith the Lord. For this is the covenant that I will make with the house of Israel after those days, saith the Lord; I will put my laws into their mind, and write them in their hearts: and I will be to them a God, and they shall be to me a people*

The promise to occupy the land will be fulfilled for the Old Testament believers, in the resurrection. The only "Zionism" that matters is Christ Jesus on His throne. The inheritance is passed to all who have put on Christ. 

**Romans 8:14–17**
*For as many as are led by the Spirit of God, they are the sons of God. For ye have not received the spirit of bondage again to fear; but ye have received the Spirit of adoption, whereby we cry, Abba, Father. The Spirit itself beareth witness with our spirit, that we are the children of God: And if children, then heirs; heirs of God, and joint-heirs with Christ; if so be that we suffer with him, that we may be also glorified together*.

This promise, the inheritance and blessings, is the heritage of all believers, from Adam and Abel, Seth and Noah, through every professing Christian today.
